# ALL ABOUT BRAIN — 1,500 Chronological Psychology History Topics (Master Topic Library)

> **Channel**: ALL ABOUT BRAIN (`@AllAboutBrainOfficial`)
> **Total Topics**: 1,500 (Strictly Chronological from c. 2600 BCE to 2026)
> **Scope**: Philosophy of Mind, Psychophysics, Experimental, Psychoanalysis, Behaviorism, Gestalt, Humanistic, Cognitive, Social, Developmental, Clinical, Personality, Industrial-Organizational, Cross-Cultural, Evolutionary, Positive, Neuropsychology & AI Psychology
> **Prompt Database**: Pre-compiled in `BCI_Shorts_Channel/database/all_about_brain_3000_prompts.db` & `psychology_1500_prompts_db.jsonl`

| 115 | 2. Medieval & Renaissance Mind | c. 1030 | Ibn al-Haytham: Optics and the Psychology of Perception | Ibn al-Haytham, Cairo Egypt | Proves visual perception involves optical physics plus unconscious cognitive inference and comparison in brain. | `experimental_psych` |
| 116 | 2. Medieval & Renaissance Mind | 1077 | Anselm of Canterbury: The Nature of Rational Will | Anselm of Canterbury, Bec Normandy | Analyzes human free will as the cognitive capacity to preserve rectitude of will for its own sake. | `philosophy_of_mind` |
| 117 | 2. Medieval & Renaissance Mind | c. 1095 | Al-Ghazali: Deliverance from Error and Skepticism | Al-Ghazali, Baghdad & Nishapur | Documents severe psychological breakdown, cognitive dissonance, and resolution via inner spiritual practice. | `cultural_psych` |
| 118 | 2. Medieval & Renaissance Mind | 1105 | Al-Ghazali: Alchemy of Happiness and Self-Knowledge | Al-Ghazali, Tus Persia | Translates spiritual psychology into practical guide for mastering animal passions and purifying the soul. | `cultural_psych` |
| 119 | 2. Medieval & Renaissance Mind | c. 1138 | Peter Abelard: Scito Te Ipsum and Moral Intention | Peter Abelard, Paris France | Argues moral guilt or virtue resides strictly in conscious mental intention rather than external action. | `philosophy_of_mind` |
| 120 | 2. Medieval & Renaissance Mind | c. 1150 | Ibn Tufayl: Hayy ibn Yaqdhan and Empirical Development | Ibn Tufayl, Islamic Spain | Philosophical novel showing feral child on desert island developing abstract reason and moral psychology. | `developmental_psych` |
| 121 | 2. Medieval & Renaissance Mind | c. 1155 | Hildegard of Bingen: Causae et Curae Holistic Healing | Hildegard of Bingen, Rupertsberg | Examines humoral melancholy, dream temperaments, and restorative musical harmony in monastic medicine. | `clinical_psych` |
| 122 | 2. Medieval & Renaissance Mind | c. 1175 | Zhu Xi: Neo-Confucian Governance of Mind and Heart | Zhu Xi, Song Dynasty China | Synthesizes human mind (Xin) as the master unifying moral principle (Li) with emotional energy (Qi). | `cultural_psych` |
| 123 | 2. Medieval & Renaissance Mind | c. 1180 | Ibn Rushd (Averroes): Unity of the Active Intellect | Ibn Rushd, Cordoba Spain | Commentary on Aristotle asserting universal active intellect illumines individual mortal human minds. | `philosophy_of_mind` |
| 124 | 2. Medieval & Renaissance Mind | c. 1190 | Maimonides: Eight Chapters on Ethics and Character Balance | Moses Maimonides, Cairo Egypt | Psychological treatise using Aristotelian golden mean to rehabilitate emotional extremes and character flaws. | `clinical_psych` |
| 125 | 2. Medieval & Renaissance Mind | 1231 | Dogen Zenji: Shobogenzo and Dropping Off Body-Mind | Eihei Dogen, Kamakura Japan | Analyzes non-dual awareness, body-mind dropping off (Shinjin Datsuraku), and lived temporal flow. | `cultural_psych` |
| 126 | 2. Medieval & Renaissance Mind | c. 1240 | Albertus Magnus: De Anima and Animal Cognition | Albertus Magnus, Cologne | Examines sensory imagination, memory faculties, and instinctual cognitive abilities in animal species. | `evolutionary_psych` |
| 127 | 2. Medieval & Renaissance Mind | 1247 | Bethlem Royal Hospital (Bedlam) Founded in London | Priory of St Mary of Bethlehem, London | Established in London; by 1403 becomes Europe's most famous custodial asylum for the mentally ill. | `clinical_psych` |
| 128 | 2. Medieval & Renaissance Mind | c. 1250 | Geel Family Foster Care System for the Mentally Ill | Shrine of St. Dymphna, Geel Belgium | Pioneers community psychiatry: local families host psychiatric patients in homes without asylum locks. | `clinical_psych` |
| 129 | 2. Medieval & Renaissance Mind | 1265 | Thomas Aquinas: The Rational and Sensory Passions | Thomas Aquinas, Univ of Paris | Summa Theologiae maps 11 primary emotional passions divided into concupiscible and irascible appetites. | `philosophy_of_mind` |
| 130 | 2. Medieval & Renaissance Mind | 1270 | Aquinas: Soul as the Unifying Substantial Form of Body | Thomas Aquinas, Rome | Rejects dualism, asserting human being is unified composite where bodily sensations nourish intellect. | `philosophy_of_mind` |
| 131 | 2. Medieval & Renaissance Mind | c. 1280 | Bacon: Idols of the Mind Precursors in Opus Majus | Roger Bacon, Oxford | Identifies four barriers to truth: fragile authority, custom, popular prejudice, and false conceit of knowledge. | `cognitive_psych` |
| 132 | 2. Medieval & Renaissance Mind | c. 1300 | Duns Scotus: Voluntarism and Primacy of the Will | John Duns Scotus, Oxford & Paris | Argues human will is autonomous and superior to intellect, capable of freely choosing contrary to reason. | `philosophy_of_mind` |
| 133 | 2. Medieval & Renaissance Mind | c. 1305 | Arnaldus de Villa Nova: Regimen for Mania and Delusion | Arnaldus de Villa Nova, Montpellier | Integrates Galenic medicine with psychological regimens to soothe acute delirium and fixed obsessions. | `clinical_psych` |
| 134 | 2. Medieval & Renaissance Mind | c. 1320 | William of Ockham: Nominalism and Mental Language | William of Ockham, Oxford | Asserts universal concepts are merely mental signs (mental language) representing individual concrete things. | `philosophy_of_mind` |
| 135 | 2. Medieval & Renaissance Mind | c. 1340 | Petrarch: Ascent of Mount Ventoux and Introspection | Petrarch, Provence France | Climbs mountain and reads Augustines Confessions at summit, sparking Renaissance focus on human inner life. | `philosophy_of_mind` |
| 136 | 2. Medieval & Renaissance Mind | c. 1350 | Jean Buridan: Buridan's Ass and Choice Paralysis | Jean Buridan, Univ of Paris | Critiques pure intellectual determinism via the paradox of a hungry donkey starving between two haystacks. | `cognitive_psych` |
| 137 | 2. Medieval & Renaissance Mind | 1377 | Ibn Khaldun: Asabiyyah and Collective Social Psychology | Ibn Khaldun, Cairo & Tunis | Muqaddimah analyzes group solidarity (Asabiyyah) as psychological glue determining rise and fall of empires. | `social_psych` |
| 138 | 2. Medieval & Renaissance Mind | 1395 | Julian of Norwich: Revelations and Existential Solace | Julian of Norwich, England | Chronicles illness-induced visions and psychological resilience against despair during the Black Death. | `positive_psych` |
| 139 | 2. Medieval & Renaissance Mind | c. 1400 | Jean Gerson: Distinguishing True Visions from Delusion | Jean Gerson, Univ of Paris | De Distinctione Verarum Visionum provides criteria to diagnose psychiatric hallucinations versus ecstasy. | `clinical_psych` |
| 140 | 2. Medieval & Renaissance Mind | 1409 | Father Joan Gilabert Jofré: Valencia Psychiatric Asylum | Hospital dels Innocents, Valencia Spain | Founds the Western world's first hospital devoted exclusively to protecting and treating the insane. | `clinical_psych` |
| 141 | 2. Medieval & Renaissance Mind | c. 1440 | Nicholas of Cusa: Learned Ignorance and Mental Limits | Nicholas of Cusa, Kues Germany | De Docta Ignorantia explores cognitive paradox that highest wisdom is knowing the boundary of ones mind. | `philosophy_of_mind` |
| 142 | 2. Medieval & Renaissance Mind | 1486 | Pico della Mirandola: Oration on Human Plasticity | Pico della Mirandola, Florence | Celebrates humanity as free sculptor of its own nature, able to sink to beasts or rise to divine minds. | `philosophy_of_mind` |
| 143 | 2. Medieval & Renaissance Mind | 1487 | Malleus Maleficarum: Demonization of Female Melancholia | Heinrich Kramer, Speyer Germany | Infamous inquisitor manual framing mental illness, trauma, and hysteria as evidence of witchcraft pacts. | `clinical_psych` |
| 144 | 2. Medieval & Renaissance Mind | 1489 | Marsilio Ficino: Three Books on Life and Genius Moods | Marsilio Ficino, Medici Academy Florence | Links Aristotelian creative genius to Saturnian melancholy, prescribing music and light therapy. | `clinical_psych` |
| 145 | 2. Medieval & Renaissance Mind | c. 1504 | Leonardo da Vinci: Hot Wax Casts of Brain Ventricles | Leonardo da Vinci, Florence & Milan | Injects melted wax into ox brain ventricles to reveal their true 3D anatomy, refuting medieval diagrams. | `neuropsychology` |
| 146 | 2. Medieval & Renaissance Mind | 1506 | Marko Marulic: Coining the Latin Term Psychologia | Marko Marulic, Split Croatia | Authors Psychologia de Ratione Animae Humanae, the earliest known work using the word Psychology. | `philosophy_of_mind` |
| 147 | 2. Medieval & Renaissance Mind | 1509 | Erasmus: The Praise of Folly and Human Self-Deception | Desiderius Erasmus, London | Satirical dissection of universal psychological defenses, narcissistic vanity, and comforting social delusions. | `social_psych` |
| 148 | 2. Medieval & Renaissance Mind | 1513 | Machiavelli: The Prince and Cold Political Psychology | Niccolo Machiavelli, Florence | Ruthless analysis of human fear, gratitude, ambition, and social manipulation in political statecraft. | `social_psych` |
| 149 | 2. Medieval & Renaissance Mind | 1517 | Wang Yangming: Unity of Moral Knowledge and Action | Wang Yangming, Ming Dynasty China | Argues innate moral intuition (Liangzhi) and behavioral action are inseparable facets of one mental act. | `cultural_psych` |
| 150 | 2. Medieval & Renaissance Mind | 1524 | Vives: De Subventione Pauperum and Social Psychology | Juan Luis Vives, Bruges | Pioneers urban welfare and rehabilitation, arguing poverty and social abandonment cause mental despair. | `social_psych` |
| 151 | 2. Medieval & Renaissance Mind | 1530 | Paracelsus: Psychosomatic Illness and Invisible Diseases | Paracelsus, Basel & Salzburg | De Morbis Invisibilibus argues imagination and psychological trauma physically manifest as somatic disease. | `clinical_psych` |
| 152 | 2. Medieval & Renaissance Mind | 1538 | Juan Luis Vives: De Anima et Vita Modern Psychology Dawn | Juan Luis Vives, Basel | Foundational work: studies association of ideas, emotion triggers, and memory recall through introspection. | `experimental_psych` |
| 153 | 2. Medieval & Renaissance Mind | 1543 | Andreas Vesalius: De Humani Corporis Fabrica Brain Atlas | Andreas Vesalius, Univ of Padua | Book VII provides detailed woodcuts of human cerebral cortex, corpus callosum, and striate nuclei. | `neuropsychology` |
| 154 | 2. Medieval & Renaissance Mind | 1563 | Johann Weyer: De Praestigiis Daemonum Defends Witches | Johann Weyer, Basel | Courageous physician proves accused witches suffer from organic melancholia and senility, not evil spells. | `clinical_psych` |
| 155 | 2. Medieval & Renaissance Mind | 1573 | Costanzo Varolio: Discovery of the Brainstem Pons | Costanzo Varolio, Bologna & Rome | Dissects the human brain from the base upward, identifying the Pons Varolii and tracing optic tracts. | `neuropsychology` |
| 156 | 2. Medieval & Renaissance Mind | 1575 | Juan Huarte: The Examination of Mens Wits | Juan Huarte de San Juan, Baeza Spain | First vocational psychology book: matches innate temperament and cognitive strengths to fitting professions. | `industrial_org` |
| 157 | 2. Medieval & Renaissance Mind | 1580 | Michel de Montaigne: Essays on Psychological Subjectivity | Michel de Montaigne, Bordeaux | Invented the essay format to explore the shifting, unstable, and deeply subjective nature of the human ego. | `philosophy_of_mind` |
| 158 | 2. Medieval & Renaissance Mind | 1584 | Giordano Bruno: The Memory Palace and Cosmic Mind | Giordano Bruno, London | De Umbris Idearum connects mnemonic architectural palaces with universal archetypes and cognitive association. | `cognitive_psych` |
| 159 | 2. Medieval & Renaissance Mind | 1586 | Timothy Bright: A Treatise of Melancholie | Timothy Bright, London | First English psychiatric treatise differentiating spiritual crises of conscience from bodily melancholia. | `clinical_psych` |
| 160 | 2. Medieval & Renaissance Mind | 1590 | Rudolf Goclenius: First Printed Book Titled Psychologia | Rudolf Goclenius, Marburg Germany | Publishes the first printed academic treatise bearing the Greek title Psychologia on human soul origin. | `philosophy_of_mind` |
| 161 | 2. Medieval & Renaissance Mind | c. 1600 | William Shakespeare: Hamlet Melancholy and Guilt | William Shakespeare, Globe Theatre | Profound dramatization of psychological paralysis, mourning, simulated madness, and existential dread. | `clinical_psych` |
| 162 | 2. Medieval & Renaissance Mind | 1605 | Shakespeare: Macbeth and the Hallucinations of Guilt | William Shakespeare, London | Portrays psychological unraveling: PTSD insomnia, visual projection of dagger, and guilt-induced psychosis. | `clinical_psych` |
| 163 | 2. Medieval & Renaissance Mind | 1621 | Robert Burton: The Anatomy of Melancholy | Robert Burton, Oxford University | Monumental 900-page compendium analyzing biological, social, love, and religious causes of clinical depression. | `clinical_psych` |
| 164 | 2. Medieval & Renaissance Mind | 1628 | William Harvey: Emotional Heart Rate Circulatory Effects | William Harvey, London | De Motu Cordis observes grief, joy, and panic directly alter circulatory heart hemodynamics via nervous link. | `neuropsychology` |
| 165 | 2. Medieval & Renaissance Mind | 1637 | René Descartes: Discours de la Methode and Cogito | René Descartes, Leiden | Formulates Cogito Ergo Sum (I think therefore I am) establishing conscious thought as bedrock of certainty. | `philosophy_of_mind` |

## 3. Enlightenment Philosophy (#166 – #260)

| # | Era | Year / Period | Topic Title | Key Figure(s) / Institution | 1-Sentence Core Summary | Subfield |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 166 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1641 | Descartes: Meditations on First Philosophy and Dualism | René Descartes, Paris | Rigid substance dualism: unextended thinking mind (Res Cogitans) vs extended mechanical body (Res Extensa). | `philosophy_of_mind` |
| 167 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1644 | Pierre Gassendi: Empiricist Rebuttal to Cartesian Mind | Pierre Gassendi, Paris | Challenges Descartes' innate ideas, arguing all mental concepts derive from material sensory impressions. | `philosophy_of_mind` |
| 168 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1649 | Descartes: The Passions of the Soul and Pineal Gland | René Descartes, Amsterdam | Theorizes passions are caused by animal spirits vibrating the central pineal gland between mind and brain. | `philosophy_of_mind` |
| 169 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1651 | Thomas Hobbes: Leviathan and Psychological Egoism | Thomas Hobbes, London | Asserts human behavior is driven mechanistically by appetite and aversion, with life solitary, poor, and brutish. | `social_psych` |
| 170 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1662 | Blaise Pascal: Pensées and Reasons of the Heart | Blaise Pascal, Port-Royal Paris | Declares the heart has reasons that reason cannot know, analyzing existential distraction and belief. | `philosophy_of_mind` |
| 171 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1662 | Descartes Treatise of Man: Automated Reflex Arc Model | René Descartes, Published Posthumously | Illustrates foot touching fire pulling neural filament to release animal spirits into muscle, causing reflex. | `experimental_psych` |
| 172 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1664 | Thomas Willis: Cerebri Anatome and Coining Neurology | Thomas Willis & Christopher Wren, Oxford | Coins the term Neurology, maps the Circle of Willis, and relocates higher cognition to cerebral cortex. | `neuropsychology` |
| 173 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1665 | La Rochefoucauld: Maxims on Hidden Egoistic Motives | Francois de La Rochefoucauld, Paris | Penetrating aphorisms exposing how unconscious vanity and self-love disguise themselves as noble virtue. | `social_psych` |
| 174 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1672 | Thomas Willis: Brain Origin of Hysteria in De Anima | Thomas Willis, Oxford & London | Refutes uterine etiology by proving hysteria and melancholia are disorders of the brain and nerves. | `neuropsychology` |
| 175 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1674 | Nicolas Malebranche: Apparent Distance and Moon Illusion | Nicolas Malebranche, Paris | Explains the horizon moon illusion via unconscious size-distance scaling over intervening terrain cues. | `cognitive_psych` |
| 176 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1677 | Baruch Spinoza: Ethics and Affective Conatus | Baruch Spinoza, The Hague | Rejects dualism for pantheist monism: mind and body are attributes of one substance; Conatus drives life. | `philosophy_of_mind` |
| 177 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1686 | Spinoza: Emotions Transformed via Adequate Understanding | Baruch Spinoza, Opera Posthuma | Argues irrational destructive passions become harmless joy once mind rationally perceives their causes. | `clinical_psych` |
| 178 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1689 | John Locke: Tabula Rasa in Human Understanding | John Locke, London | Rejects innate ideas; argues all concepts originate exclusively from sensory experience and reflection. | `philosophy_of_mind` |
| 179 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1690 | Locke: Personal Identity Grounded in Conscious Memory | John Locke, London | Pioneering psychological claim: personal identity extends only as far back as conscious memory reaches. | `cognitive_psych` |
| 180 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1693 | John Locke: Some Thoughts Concerning Education | John Locke, London | Recommends positive reinforcement, gentle character shaping, and avoidance of corporal terror in schooling. | `developmental_psych` |
| 181 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1695 | Molyneux Problem: Visual Perception in Cured Blindness | William Molyneux & John Locke | Asks whether a person born blind, suddenly made to see, could distinguish sphere from cube by sight alone. | `cognitive_psych` |
| 182 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1704 | Leibniz: Petites Perceptions and the Subconscious Mind | Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz, Hanover | New Essays proposes threshold of consciousness: countless tiny unconscious impressions accumulate into awareness. | `cognitive_psych` |
| 183 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1707 | Georg Ernst Stahl: Animism and Psychogenic Disease | Georg Ernst Stahl, Univ of Halle | Argues the sensitive soul (Anima) directly regulates bodily vascular tone, causing somatic illness. | `clinical_psych` |
| 184 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1709 | George Berkeley: An Essay Towards a New Theory of Vision | George Berkeley, Dublin | Argues distance perception is not innate geometry but learned association between visual and tactile cues. | `cognitive_psych` |
| 185 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1710 | Berkeley: Esse est Percipi (To Be is to Be Perceived) | George Berkeley, Dublin | Radical subjective idealism: physical reality exists solely as perceived mental sensory ideas in conscious minds. | `philosophy_of_mind` |
| 186 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1714 | Bernard Mandeville: Fable of the Bees and Private Vices | Bernard Mandeville, London | Argues private selfish psychological vices (greed, envy, pride) paradoxically drive economic public benefits. | `social_psych` |
| 187 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1725 | Francis Hutcheson: The Innate Moral Sense Theory | Francis Hutcheson, Glasgow | Proposes humans possess an innate moral sense generating disinterested benevolence and pleasure in virtue. | `social_psych` |
| 188 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1725 | Giambattista Vico: Scienza Nuova and Cultural Mind | Giambattista Vico, Naples Italy | Pioneers cultural psychology: human minds construct shared historical meaning through myth and metaphor. | `cultural_psych` |
| 189 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1726 | Joseph Butler: Sermons on Human Nature and Conscience | Bishop Joseph Butler, London | Examines how conscience naturally holds legitimate psychological supremacy over passions and cool self-love. | `philosophy_of_mind` |
| 190 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1728 | William Cheselden: Cataract Surgery Tests Molyneux | William Cheselden, St Thomas' Hospital | Restores sight to a boy born blind, proving he could not initially recognize 3D shapes by vision alone. | `experimental_psych` |
| 191 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1733 | George Cheyne: The English Malady on Nervous Spleen | George Cheyne, London & Bath | Attributes rising melancholia and nervous exhaustion among British elites to urban stress and diet. | `clinical_psych` |
| 192 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1734 | Christian Wolff: Coining Empirical and Rational Psychology | Christian Wolff, Marburg | Formalizes academic division between empirical psychology (observation) and rational psychology (mind essence). | `experimental_psych` |
| 193 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1738 | Daniel Bernoulli: Specimen Theoriae Novae and Expected | Daniel Bernoulli, St. Petersburg | Introduces logarithmic subjective utility of wealth to resolve the famous St. Petersburg paradox. | `cognitive_psych` |
| 194 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1739 | David Hume: A Treatise of Human Nature | David Hume, Edinburgh | Declares reason is, and ought only to be the slave of the passions, unable to motivate action on its own. | `philosophy_of_mind` |
| 195 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1740 | Hume: The Bundle Theory of the Elusive Self | David Hume, Treatise Book I | Introspecting reveals only fleeting impressions of heat, joy, or pain; there is no fixed permanent self. | `philosophy_of_mind` |
| 196 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1746 | Condillac: Origin of Human Knowledge and Signs | Etienne Bonnot de Condillac, Paris | Demonstrates how arbitrary linguistic signs transform passive sensory associations into active reflection. | `cognitive_psych` |
| 197 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1748 | Julien Offray de La Mettrie: LHomme Machine | Julien Offray de La Mettrie, Leiden | Extends Descartes animal machine to humans: mind is strictly physiological machinery of organized brain matter. | `philosophy_of_mind` |
| 198 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1749 | David Hartley: Observations on Man and Neural Vibrations | David Hartley, London | First physiological associationism: claims nerve vibrations (vibratiuncles) encode and chain memory ideas. | `experimental_psych` |
| 199 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1751 | Denis Diderot: Letter on the Deaf and Dumb | Denis Diderot, Paris | Analyzes origin of language, symbolic thought, and sensory compensation in individuals born deaf or blind. | `cognitive_psych` |
| 200 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1752 | Albrecht von Haller: Nerve Sensibility vs Irritability | Albrecht von Haller, Gottingen | Experimental physiology distinguishes autonomous muscle contraction from conscious nerve sensibility. | `experimental_psych` |
| 201 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1754 | Etienne de Condillac: Treatise on the Sensations | Etienne Bonnot de Condillac, Paris | Imagines a lifeless marble statue awakened to human intellect and emotion solely through the single sense of smell. | `philosophy_of_mind` |
| 202 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1755 | Rousseau: Discourse on Inequality and the Noble Savage | Jean-Jacques Rousseau, Geneva | Argues humans in natural state were peaceful and compassionate; civilization bred vanity, greed, and neurosis. | `cultural_psych` |
| 203 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1756 | Edmund Burke: The Sublime and the Psychology of Terror | Edmund Burke, London | Defines the Sublime as awe and profound aesthetic delight provoked by safe contemplation of terror and infinity. | `philosophy_of_mind` |
| 204 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1758 | William Battie: A Treatise on Madness and Reform | William Battie, St Luke's Hospital London | Separates congenital from treatable madness and advocates humane medical care over Bedlam exhibitions. | `clinical_psych` |
| 205 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1759 | Adam Smith: Theory of Moral Sentiments and Sympathy | Adam Smith, Glasgow | Identifies mutual sympathy as foundation of morality, introducing the internal Impartial Spectator conscience. | `social_psych` |
| 206 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1760 | Charles Bonnet: Visual Hallucinations in the Blind | Charles Bonnet, Geneva Switzerland | Describes his grandfather's vivid, silent visual hallucinations of figures following cataract vision loss. | `neuropsychology` |
| 207 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1762 | Jean-Jacques Rousseau: Emile and Child Development | Jean-Jacques Rousseau, Paris | Pioneers child-centered education, arguing children progress through distinct developmental cognitive stages. | `developmental_psych` |
| 208 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1764 | Thomas Reid: An Inquiry into the Human Mind | Thomas Reid, Aberdeen | Founders Scottish Common Sense school, refuting Humes skepticism by asserting trust in direct perception. | `philosophy_of_mind` |
| 209 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1765 | Leibniz: Posthumous Publication of Nouveaux Essais | Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz, Amsterdam | Rebuttal to Lockes tabula rasa: the mind contains innate veins like marble, predisposed to certain truths. | `philosophy_of_mind` |
| 210 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1766 | Moses Mendelssohn: Letters on the Sensations | Moses Mendelssohn, Berlin | Explores psychological pleasures of beauty, mixed emotions, and human capacity for aesthetic sublimity. | `philosophy_of_mind` |
| 211 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1769 | William Cullen: Coining the Medical Term Neurosis | William Cullen, Edinburgh University | Classifies physical and mental illnesses originating from functional disturbance of the nervous system. | `clinical_psych` |
| 212 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1771 | Johann August Unzer: Sensory-Motor Reflex Physiology | Johann August Unzer, Rinteln Germany | Introduces afferent-to-efferent neural reflex action occurring independently of conscious awareness. | `neuropsychology` |
| 213 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1772 | Johann Gottfried Herder: Origin of Language and Volk | Johann Gottfried Herder, Strasbourg | Argues reflective awareness (Besonnenheit) and language co-evolve within distinct cultural folk spirits. | `cultural_psych` |
| 214 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1774 | Franz Anton Mesmer: Animal Magnetism and Induced Trance | Franz Anton Mesmer, Vienna | Claims magnetic fluid flows through bodies; induces hypnotic crises in patients via iron tubs and wands. | `clinical_psych` |
| 215 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1775 | Johann Kaspar Lavater: Essays on Physiognomy | Johann Kaspar Lavater, Zurich | Claims facial profiles and skull silhouettes directly expose internal psychological character and moral soul. | `social_psych` |
| 216 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1777 | Johann Nikolaus Tetens: Tripartite Mind Classification | Johann Nikolaus Tetens, Kiel Germany | Establishes the classic modern division of mental faculties into Cognition, Affect (Feeling), and Will. | `philosophy_of_mind` |
| 217 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1778 | Mesmer Arrives in Paris: High Society Hypnosis Craze | Franz Anton Mesmer, Paris | Parisian aristocracy flocks to Mesmers magnetic salons, experiencing mass hysterical swooning and cures. | `social_psych` |
| 218 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1781 | Immanuel Kant: Critique of Pure Reason | Immanuel Kant, Konigsberg | Argues mind actively constructs reality using a priori categories of space, time, causality, and substance. | `philosophy_of_mind` |
| 219 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1783 | Karl Philipp Moritz: First Empirical Psychology Journal | Karl Philipp Moritz, Berlin | Launches Gnothi Sauton, the world's first periodical devoted to clinical case histories and dreams. | `clinical_psych` |
| 220 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1784 | Franklin Commission on Mesmerism: Exposing Placebo | Franklin, Lavoisier & Guillotin | Blindfolded trials prove animal magnetism effects vanish when patient lacks expectation, proving placebo. | `experimental_psych` |
| 221 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1784 | Georg Prochaska: Sensorium Commune and Reflex Law | Georg Prochaska, Vienna & Prague | Defines the brainstem Sensorium Commune as the automatic reflex locus serving organism self-preservation. | `neuropsychology` |

| 223 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1785 | Thomas Reid: Essays on the Intellectual Powers of Man | Thomas Reid, Glasgow | Detailed faculty psychology cataloging attention, perception, judgment, memory, and moral taste. | `cognitive_psych` |
| 224 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1786 | Kant: Metaphysical Foundations and Psychology Denial | Immanuel Kant, Riga | Asserts psychology can never become a true exact science because mental states lack spatial dimension and math. | `philosophy_of_mind` |
| 225 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1788 | Alexander Crichton: An Inquiry into Mental Derangement | Alexander Crichton, London | Provides early clinical description of morbid restlessness and attention deficit disorder in children. | `clinical_psych` |
| 226 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1789 | Jeremy Bentham: Principles of Morals and Felicific Calculus | Jeremy Bentham, London | Proposes mathematical calculus measuring intensity, duration, and certainty of psychological pleasure and pain. | `cognitive_psych` |
| 227 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1790 | Immanuel Kant: Critique of Judgment and Aesthetic Mind | Immanuel Kant, Konigsberg | Analyzes psychological pleasure of purposiveness without purpose in fine art and sublime landscapes. | `philosophy_of_mind` |
| 228 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1791 | Luigi Galvani: Animal Electricity in Frog Nerves | Luigi Galvani, Univ of Bologna | Discovers frog muscles contract when nerves are touched by metal arcs, proving bioelectric signaling. | `neuropsychology` |
| 229 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1792 | Mary Wollstonecraft: Vindication of the Rights of Woman | Mary Wollstonecraft, London | Argues perceived female emotional weakness is psychological artifact of denied education and socialization. | `social_psych` |
| 230 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1793 | Philippe Pinel: Unchaining the Insane at Bicetre Asylum | Philippe Pinel, Paris | Orders heavy iron chains removed from asylum inmates, introducing humane moral therapy and counseling. | `clinical_psych` |
| 231 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1794 | Erasmus Darwin: Zoonomia and Organic Psychology | Erasmus Darwin, London | Formulates early evolutionary theory of mental habits, instincts, and animal sensations. | `evolutionary_psych` |
| 232 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1796 | Franz Joseph Gall: Lectures on Cranioscopy in Vienna | Franz Joseph Gall, Vienna | Pioneers phrenology: brain consists of distinct cortical organs whose size shapes skull contours. | `neuropsychology` |
| 233 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1796 | Nevil Maskelyne: Greenwich Transit Reaction Time Lag | Nevil Maskelyne, Greenwich Observatory | Fires assistant Kinnebrook for systematic 0.8-second stellar timing lag, sparking reaction time research. | `experimental_psych` |
| 234 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1796 | William Tuke: Founding the York Retreat Asylum | William Tuke, York England | Quaker retreat provides quiet rural haven without restraints, pioneering modern therapeutic milieu. | `clinical_psych` |
| 235 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1798 | Immanuel Kant: Anthropology from a Pragmatic Point of View | Immanuel Kant, Konigsberg | Final major work surveying human personality types, cognitive biases, mental disorders, and ethnic customs. | `philosophy_of_mind` |
| 236 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1798 | John Haslam: Observations on Insanity and Paresis | John Haslam, Bethlem Royal Hospital | Documents clinical delusion of grandiosity and progressive dementia in psychiatric hospital inmates. | `clinical_psych` |
| 237 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1800 | Jean-Marc-Gaspard Itard: The Wild Boy of Aveyron | Jean-Marc-Gaspard Itard, Paris | Attempts five-year sensory education of feral child Victor, pioneering special education and language limits. | `developmental_psych` |
| 238 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1801 | Pinel: Medico-Philosophical Treatise on Alienation | Philippe Pinel, Paris | Classifies mental illness into mania, melancholia, dementia, and idiotism without organic delirium. | `clinical_psych` |
| 239 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1802 | Thomas Young: Three-Color Retinal Receptor Lecture | Thomas Young, Royal Society London | Proposes the human retina requires only three color-sensitive resonators to perceive the full spectrum. | `experimental_psych` |
| 240 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1803 | Johann Christian Reil: Coining the Term Psychiatry | Johann Christian Reil, Halle Germany | Authors Rhapsodies on psychic treatment and coins Psychiatrie (1808) as a core medical specialty. | `clinical_psych` |
| 241 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1806 | Fichte: Addresses to the German Nation on Pedagogical Will | Johann Gottlieb Fichte, Berlin | Advocates education system explicitly designed to train autonomous moral will and collective identity. | `developmental_psych` |
| 242 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1807 | Hegel: Phenomenology of Spirit and Master-Slave Dialectic | G.W.F. Hegel, Jena | Explores psychological struggle for mutual recognition (Anerkennung) between conflicting conscious egos. | `philosophy_of_mind` |
| 243 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1810 | Johann Wolfgang von Goethe: Theory of Colours | Goethe, Weimar | Analyzes subjective optical afterimages, colored shadows, and emotional psychological impact of color. | `experimental_psych` |
| 244 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1811 | Charles Bell: Motor vs Sensory Spinal Nerve Roots | Sir Charles Bell, London | Demonstrates anterior spinal nerve roots govern motor movement while posterior roots carry sensation. | `neuropsychology` |
| 245 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1812 | Benjamin Rush: Medical Inquiries and Diseases of the Mind | Benjamin Rush, Philadelphia | Father of American psychiatry publishes first textbook, designing the gyrator and tranquilizer chair. | `clinical_psych` |
| 246 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1816 | Johann Friedrich Herbart: Text-Book in Psychology | Johann Friedrich Herbart, Konigsberg | Proposes mathematical models of competing ideas and the Apperceptive Mass threshold of consciousness. | `experimental_psych` |
| 247 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1817 | James Parkinson: An Essay on the Shaking Palsy | James Parkinson, London | Clinical monograph detailing motor tremors, festinating gait, and cognitive preservation in paralysis agitans. | `neuropsychology` |
| 248 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1818 | Arthur Schopenhauer: The Primacy of the Blind Will | Arthur Schopenhauer, Dresden | The World as Will and Representation: human intellect is mere servant to blind, irrational unconscious drive. | `philosophy_of_mind` |
| 249 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1820 | Thomas Brown: Lectures on the Philosophy of the Human Mind | Thomas Brown, Edinburgh | Refines associationism with secondary laws: duration, liveliness, recency, and constitutional habits. | `cognitive_psych` |
| 250 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1822 | Francois Magendie: Dorsal-Ventral Spinal Root Proof | Francois Magendie, Collège de France | Experimental spinal root sectioning in animals conclusively proves the Bell-Magendie law of nerve paths. | `neuropsychology` |
| 251 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1823 | Friedrich Bessel: The Personal Equation in Astronomy | Friedrich Wilhelm Bessel, Konigsberg | Quantifies systematic individual differences in visual-auditory reaction times among trained astronomers. | `experimental_psych` |
| 252 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1824 | Herbart: Psychology as a Science Founded on Experience | Johann Friedrich Herbart, Konigsberg | Declares psychology must use mathematics to track force and resistance of conscious and suppressed ideas. | `experimental_psych` |
| 253 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1824 | Pierre Flourens: Cortical Ablation and Brain Plasticity | Marie-Jean-Pierre Flourens, Paris | Debunks phrenology via surgical lesions, showing cortex mediates perception while cerebellum guides motion. | `neuropsychology` |
| 254 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1825 | Jan Evangelista Purkinje: Twilight Color Brightness Shift | Jan Evangelista Purkinje, Breslau | Observes peak spectral brightness shifts toward blue-green at dusk as retinal rods replace cones. | `experimental_psych` |
| 255 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1834 | Ernst Heinrich Weber: De Tactu and Webers Law | Ernst Heinrich Weber, Leipzig | Discovers just noticeable difference (JND) is a constant ratio of original stimulus intensity (delta I / I = k). | `experimental_psych` |
| 256 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1835 | James Cowles Prichard: Moral Insanity and Psychopathy | James Cowles Prichard, Bristol England | Defines Moral Insanity where patients show intact intellect but severe affective and impulse distortion. | `clinical_psych` |
| 257 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1838 | Jean-Etienne Esquirol: Hallucination vs Illusion Defined | Jean-Etienne Dominique Esquirol, Paris | Separates true hallucinations (perception without external object) from sensory illusions and monomania. | `clinical_psych` |
| 258 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1838 | Johannes Muller: Doctrine of Specific Nerve Energies | Johannes Peter Muller, Berlin | Proves sensory modality depends on which sensory nerve is stimulated, not on the physical stimulus itself. | `experimental_psych` |
| 259 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1843 | John Stuart Mill: A System of Logic and Mental Chemistry | John Stuart Mill, London | Formulates mental chemistry: complex cognitive ideas fuse into emergent properties exceeding sum of parts. | `philosophy_of_mind` |
| 260 | 3. Enlightenment Philosophy | 1844 | Søren Kierkegaard: The Concept of Anxiety (Angst) | Søren Kierkegaard, Copenhagen | Masterwork exploring existential dread as the dizziness of human freedom confronting infinite possibilities. | `philosophy_of_mind` |

## 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology (#261 – #420)

| # | Era | Year / Period | Topic Title | Key Figure(s) / Institution | 1-Sentence Core Summary | Subfield |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 261 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1846 | Weber: Der Tastsinn und das Gemeingefuhl | Ernst Heinrich Weber, Leipzig | Systematic tactile discrimination thresholds mapping spatial resolution across the human skin. | `experimental_psych` |
| 262 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1847 | Pierre Flourens: Stages of Ether Anesthesia on Brain | Pierre Flourens, French Academy Paris | Demonstrates inhaled ether progressively suspends cerebral lobes, cerebellum, and spinal reflexes. | `neuropsychology` |
| 263 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1848 | Emil du Bois-Reymond: Nerve Action Potential Recorded | Emil du Bois-Reymond, Berlin | Uses sensitive galvanometers to detect the negative electrical variation wave traveling along axons. | `neuropsychology` |
| 264 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1848 | Phineas Gage: Frontal Lobe Damage and Personality Shift | John Martyn Harlow, Vermont | Tamping iron injury destroys orbitofrontal cortex, turning gentle foreman into profane and impulsive patient. | `neuropsychology` |
| 265 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1850 | Helmholtz: Measuring Speed of the Neural Impulse | Hermann von Helmholtz, Konigsberg | Proves nerve conduction is not instantaneous, measuring nerve velocity at approximately 30 meters per second. | `experimental_psych` |
| 266 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1850 | Hermann von Helmholtz: Invention of Ophthalmoscope | Hermann von Helmholtz, Konigsberg | Enables direct visual inspection of the living human retina and optic disc for visual psychophysics. | `experimental_psych` |
| 267 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1851 | Alfonso Corti: Discovery of Cochlear Organ of Corti | Marchese Alfonso Corti, Wurzburg | Identifies cochlear hair cells on the basilar membrane that transduce sound waves into neural signals. | `neuropsychology` |
| 268 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1851 | Schopenhauer: Parerga and Paralipomena Psychology | Arthur Schopenhauer, Frankfurt | Popular essays exploring psychological vanity, cynicism, boredom, and human existential motivations. | `philosophy_of_mind` |
| 269 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1852 | Charles Wheatstone: Binocular Disparity and Stereoscope | Sir Charles Wheatstone, King's College London | Proves horizontal retinal disparity between the two eyes fuses flat images into vivid 3D depth. | `experimental_psych` |
| 270 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1852 | Lotze: Medicinische Psychologie and Local Signs | Rudolf Hermann Lotze, Gottingen | Introduces local signs (Lokalzeichen): non-spatial sensory cues translated into spatial mental perception. | `philosophy_of_mind` |
| 271 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1852 | Young-Helmholtz Trichromatic Theory of Color Vision | Hermann von Helmholtz, Berlin | Proposes human retina contains three distinct photoreceptor mechanisms sensitive to red, green, and blue. | `experimental_psych` |
| 272 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1853 | William Carpenter: The Ideomotor Reflex Phenomenon | William Benjamin Carpenter, London | Proves involuntary unconscious muscular twitches drive Ouija boards, dowsing rods, and table-turning. | `experimental_psych` |
| 273 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1854 | Baillarger & Falret: Folie Circulaire Bipolar Cycle | Jules Baillarger & Jean-Pierre Falret, Paris | Independently define circular insanity as a continuous psychiatric cycle of mania and melancholia. | `clinical_psych` |
| 274 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1854 | George Boole: Investigation of the Laws of Thought | George Boole, Queen's College Cork | Formalizes binary Boolean algebra as the mathematical calculus governing human logical deduction. | `ai_psych` |
| 275 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1855 | Alexander Bain: The Senses and the Intellect | Alexander Bain, Aberdeen | Integrates sensory physiology and associationism, publishing first textbook dedicated solely to psychology. | `philosophy_of_mind` |
| 276 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1855 | Herbert Spencer: Evolutionary Principles of Psychology | Herbert Spencer, London | Argues mental associations and instincts evolve through continuous adaptation of inner to outer relations. | `evolutionary_psych` |
| 277 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1856 | Helmholtz: Handbook of Physiological Optics Volume 1 | Hermann von Helmholtz, Leipzig | Examines corneal refraction, lens accommodation, and optical geometry of the human eye. | `experimental_psych` |
| 278 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1856 | Johann Joseph Oppel: Geometrical-Optical Space Illusion | Johann Joseph Oppel, Frankfurt | Coins geometrical-optical illusions, showing a subdivided line appears longer than an empty space. | `experimental_psych` |
| 279 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1857 | Bénédict Morel: Degeneracy and Démence Précoce Coined | Bénédict Augustin Morel, Rouen France | Introduces psychiatric degeneration theory and coins démence précoce for adolescent cognitive decline. | `clinical_psych` |
| 280 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1858 | Wilhelm Wundt: Muscular Sense and Tactile Localization | Wilhelm Wundt, Univ of Heidelberg | Studies how muscular movement sensations and cutaneous touch combine to construct spatial awareness. | `experimental_psych` |
| 281 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1859 | Alexander Bain: The Emotions and the Will | Alexander Bain, Aberdeen | Analyzes trial-and-error motor habits and spontaneous action, directly anticipating Thorndikes law of effect. | `philosophy_of_mind` |
| 282 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1859 | Charles Darwin: On the Origin of Species Published | Charles Darwin, London | Natural selection anchors modern evolutionary psychology, proving animal and human mental continuity. | `evolutionary_psych` |
| 283 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1859 | Lazarus & Steinthal: Founding Völkerpsychologie Journal | Moritz Lazarus & Heymann Steinthal, Berlin | Launch the first journal of cultural psychology analyzing shared language, myth, and folk mentality. | `cultural_psych` |
| 284 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1860 | Delboeuf Illusion: Concentric Circles Contrast | Franz Joseph Delboeuf, Liege | Discovers size-contrast optical illusion altering perceived diameter of identical target circles. | `experimental_psych` |
| 285 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1860 | Gustav Fechner: Elemente der Psychophysik Published | Gustav Fechner, Leipzig | Landmark founding of psychophysics: formulates Fechners law (S = k log R) uniting mind and physical stimuli. | `experimental_psych` |
| 286 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1860 | Johann Zöllner: The Zöllner Parallel Line Illusion | Johann Karl Friedrich Zöllner, Leipzig | Discovers parallel lines crossed by short diagonal hatches appear dramatically tilted and convergent. | `experimental_psych` |
| 287 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1861 | Ewald Hering: Law of Equal Binocular Eye Innervation | Ewald Hering, Leipzig & Prague | Proves both eyes receive symmetrical neural motor commands as if steered by a single cyclopean eye. | `experimental_psych` |
| 288 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1861 | Ewald Hering: Opponent Process Theory of Color Vision | Ewald Hering, Prague | Proposes visual system processes color in antagonistic pairs: red-green, blue-yellow, and black-white. | `experimental_psych` |
| 289 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1861 | Paul Broca: Localizing Articulated Speech in Frontal Lobe | Paul Broca, Paris | Autopsy of patient Tan reveals lesion in left inferior frontal gyrus causing expressive motor aphasia. | `neuropsychology` |
| 290 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1862 | Guillaume Duchenne: Electrophysiology of the True Smile | Guillaume-Benjamin Duchenne, Paris | Uses electrical probes to show genuine joy contracts both zygomaticus major and orbicularis oculi. | `experimental_psych` |
| 291 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1862 | Wilhelm Wundt: Contributions to Sensory Perception | Wilhelm Wundt, Heidelberg | Outlines roadmap for experimental psychology using psychological instruments and controlled introspection. | `experimental_psych` |
| 292 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1863 | Helmholtz: On the Sensations of Tone Psychoacoustics | Hermann von Helmholtz, Heidelberg | Monumental acoustic study explaining harmonic overtones, consonance, dissonance beats, and timbre. | `experimental_psych` |
| 293 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1863 | Ivan Sechenov: Reflexes of the Brain Published | Ivan Sechenov, St. Petersburg | Foundational Russian reflexology: asserts all conscious mental acts are physiological inhibitory reflex arcs. | `neuropsychology` |
| 294 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1863 | Wilhelm Wundt: Lectures on Human and Animal Psychology | Wilhelm Wundt, Heidelberg | Early popular lectures exploring comparative animal behavior, sensory illusions, and mental reaction times. | `experimental_psych` |
| 295 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1864 | Hughlings Jackson: Evolution and Dissolution of Brain | John Hughlings Jackson, London | Hierarchical nervous model: disease causes dissolution of higher evolutionary control, releasing primitive acts. | `neuropsychology` |
| 296 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1865 | Claude Bernard: Milieu Intérieur Internal Stability | Claude Bernard, Collège de France Paris | Establishes that physiological and neural regulation of the internal environment enables free action. | `neuropsychology` |
| 297 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1865 | Francis Galton: Hereditary Talent and Character | Francis Galton, London | Macmillans Magazine essays argue genius and moral qualities run in families, initiating twin research. | `evolutionary_psych` |
| 298 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1866 | Ambroise-Auguste Liébeault: Founding the Nancy School | Ambroise-Auguste Liébeault, Nancy France | Pioneers verbal hypnotic suggestion in rural clinic, proving hypnosis is psychological, not magnetic. | `clinical_psych` |
| 299 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1866 | Helmholtz: Treatise on Physiological Optics Volume 3 | Hermann von Helmholtz, Leipzig | Formulates doctrine of unconscious inference: retinal sensations interpreted via prior learned experience. | `cognitive_psych` |
| 300 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1866 | John Langdon Down: Clinical Description of Trisomy 21 | John Langdon Down, Earlswood Asylum Surrey | Provides the first systematic clinical characterization of congenital Down syndrome at Earlswood. | `clinical_psych` |
| 301 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1867 | Helmholtz: Resonance Place Theory of Auditory Hearing | Hermann von Helmholtz, Berlin | Proposes basilar membrane fibers resonate like piano strings to specific incoming acoustic frequencies. | `experimental_psych` |
| 302 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1867 | Henry Maudsley: The Physiology and Pathology of Mind | Henry Maudsley, London | Materialist psychiatry treatise grounding mental breakdowns in cerebral degeneration and heredity. | `clinical_psych` |
| 303 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1868 | Franciscus Donders: Subtractive Mental Chronometry | Franciscus Donders, Utrecht | Measures precise duration of human cognitive processes (choice and discrimination) by subtracting reaction times. | `experimental_psych` |
| 304 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1868 | Karl Vierordt: The Time Sense and Vierordt's Law | Karl von Vierordt, Univ of Tubingen | Chronometric experiments prove humans overestimate short durations and underestimate long durations. | `experimental_psych` |
| 305 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1869 | Francis Galton: Hereditary Genius Published | Francis Galton, London | Applies statistical normal distributions to human intellectual ability, coining nature versus nurture. | `experimental_psych` |
| 306 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1869 | George Miller Beard: Coining Neurasthenia Syndrome | George Miller Beard, New York City | Diagnoses Neurasthenia as central nervous exhaustion caused by the pace of modern industrial society. | `clinical_psych` |
| 307 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1870 | Fritsch and Hitzig: Electrical Excitability of Motor Cortex | Gustav Fritsch & Eduard Hitzig | Galvanic stimulation of exposed canine cortex proves localized motor control of contralateral limbs. | `neuropsychology` |
| 308 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1871 | Charles Darwin: The Descent of Man and Sexual Selection | Charles Darwin, London | Analyzes evolutionary psychology of female mate choice, male competition, and aesthetic mental preferences. | `evolutionary_psych` |
| 309 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1871 | Silas Weir Mitchell: Coining Phantom Limb Syndrome | Silas Weir Mitchell, Philadelphia | Documents vivid sensory awareness and pain in missing limbs among 90 American Civil War amputees. | `neuropsychology` |
| 310 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1872 | Charles Darwin: Expression of Emotions in Man and Animals | Charles Darwin, London | Universal facial expressions of fear, grief, and disgust prove phylogenetic evolutionary roots of emotion. | `evolutionary_psych` |
| 311 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1872 | Ernst Mach: Spatial Vision and Mach Bands Contrast | Ernst Mach, Graz Austria | Demonstrates lateral inhibition enhancement of visual borders between adjacent shades of gray. | `experimental_psych` |
| 312 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1873 | Camillo Golgi: The Black Reaction Silver Neuron Stain | Camillo Golgi, Abbiategrasso Italy | Discovers silver chromate staining (la reazione nera), making complete individual neurons visible. | `neuropsychology` |
| 313 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1873 | Francis Galton: Visualizing Mental Number Lines | Francis Galton, London | First clinical investigation of synesthesia: discovers individuals visualize numbers in fixed spatial forms. | `cognitive_psych` |
| 314 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1873 | Wilhelm Wundt: Principles of Physiological Psychology | Wilhelm Wundt, Heidelberg | Authoritative handbook establishing experimental psychology as an independent scientific discipline. | `experimental_psych` |
| 315 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1873 | William Gull & Charles Lasègue: Anorexia Nervosa Named | William Gull (London) & Lasègue (Paris) | Independently publish the first clinical descriptions of psychogenic self-starvation in young women. | `clinical_psych` |
| 316 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1874 | Carl Wernicke: Sensory Receptive Aphasia Discovery | Carl Wernicke, Breslau | Identifies left posterior superior temporal gyrus lesion causing severe language comprehension deficit. | `neuropsychology` |
| 317 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1874 | Franz Brentano: Psychology from an Empirical Standpoint | Franz Brentano, Vienna | Foundational Act Psychology: defines mental phenomena by Intentionality (directedness toward an object). | `philosophy_of_mind` |
| 318 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1874 | Karl Ludwig Kahlbaum: Clinical Monograph on Catatonia | Karl Ludwig Kahlbaum, Gorlitz Germany | Identifies Catatonia as a motor-psychiatric syndrome marked by mutism, negativism, and waxy posture. | `clinical_psych` |
| 319 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1874 | Nishi Amane: Introduces Shinrigaku to Meiji Japan | Nishi Amane, Tokyo | Coins the Sino-Japanese term Shinrigaku, translating Western empirical psychology into East Asian thought. | `cultural_psych` |
| 320 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1875 | David Ferrier: Experimental Mapping of Monkey Cortex | David Ferrier, King College London | Faradic stimulation charts precise sensory and motor topographic homunculus in non-human primates. | `neuropsychology` |
| 321 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1875 | Francis Galton: First Twin Study on Nature and Nurture | Francis Galton, London | Pioneers behavioral genetics by comparing similar and dissimilar twins across developmental life. | `evolutionary_psych` |
| 322 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1875 | Richard Caton: First Brain Electrical Activity Recorded | Richard Caton, Liverpool Royal Infirmary | Uses a reflecting galvanometer on animal cortex to discover spontaneous brain electrical fluctuations. | `neuropsychology` |
| 323 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1875 | William James: Demonstrational Psychology Lab at Harvard | William James, Cambridge MA | Harvard establishes instructional laboratory for sensory physiology and cognitive demonstrations. | `experimental_psych` |
| 324 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1876 | Alexander Bain: Founding of Mind Journal | Alexander Bain & George Robertson | First academic journal devoted specifically to psychological and philosophical inquiries of the mind. | `experimental_psych` |
| 325 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1876 | Cesare Lombroso: The Criminal Man and Atavistic Degeneracy | Cesare Lombroso, Turin | Biological determinism claiming born criminals are evolutionary throwbacks identifiable by physical traits. | `social_psych` |
| 326 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1876 | Franz Brentano: Classification of Mental Phenomena | Franz Brentano, Vienna | Divides all conscious acts into presentations (ideas), judgments (truth), and phenomena of love and hate. | `philosophy_of_mind` |
| 327 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1877 | Adolf Kussmaul: Discovery of Word Blindness (Alexia) | Adolf Kussmaul, Strasbourg | Identifies stroke patients with intact vision and speech who lose the ability to read written words. | `neuropsychology` |
| 328 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1877 | Charles Darwin: A Biographical Sketch of an Infant | Charles Darwin, Mind Journal | Pioneering developmental diary tracking reflex emergence, motor control, and vocal expressions in his son Doddy. | `developmental_psych` |
| 329 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1878 | G. Stanley Hall: First American PhD in Psychology | G. Stanley Hall & William James, Harvard | Hall earns first doctorate in psychology in the United States under the supervision of William James. | `developmental_psych` |
| 330 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1878 | George Romanes: Animal Intelligence and Evolutionary Mind | George Romanes, London | Compiles comparative anecdotal evidence tracing continuity of animal emotions, instincts, and cognition. | `evolutionary_psych` |
| 331 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1878 | Louis-Antoine Ranvier: Myelin Nodes and Nerve Conduction | Louis-Antoine Ranvier, Collège de France | Discovers periodic nodes in axon myelin sheaths that enable rapid saltatory electrical conduction. | `neuropsychology` |
| 332 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1879 | Francis Galton: Word Association and the Subconscious | Francis Galton, London | Measures reaction latency to associative word triggers, discovering recurring memories from childhood. | `experimental_psych` |
| 333 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1879 | Louis Émile Javal: Saccadic Eye Movements in Reading | Louis Émile Javal, Sorbonne Paris | Discovers eyes do not sweep smoothly across text when reading, but jump in rapid discrete saccades. | `cognitive_psych` |
| 334 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1879 | Wilhelm Wundt: Founding of Leipzig Psychology Laboratory | Wilhelm Wundt, Univ of Leipzig | Historic birth of experimental psychology: first dedicated laboratory for scientific study of conscious mind. | `experimental_psych` |
| 335 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1879 | Wundt: Gedankenmesser and Shifting Attention Latency | Wilhelm Wundt, Leipzig | Clock-pendulum apparatus proves human mind takes approximately 1/10th second to switch sensory modalities. | `experimental_psych` |
| 336 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1880 | Breuer Treats Anna O: The Birth of the Talking Cure | Josef Breuer & Bertha Pappenheim | Pioneers cathartic method in Vienna: tracing hysterical paralysis and speech blocks back to forgotten trauma. | `clinical_psych` |
| 337 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1880 | Francis Galton: Mental Imagery Questionnaire Survey | Francis Galton, London | Asks prominent scientists to describe breakfast table recall, finding vast individual differences in mental eye. | `cognitive_psych` |
| 338 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1880 | Jean-Baptiste Gélineau: Clinical Discovery of Narcolepsy | Jean-Baptiste-Édouard Gélineau, Paris | Coins Narcolepsy for irresistible sleep attacks paired with emotion-triggered muscle tone loss. | `neuropsychology` |
| 339 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1881 | Hermann Munk: Psychic Blindness (Visual Agnosia) Mapped | Hermann Munk, Univ of Berlin | Occipital lobe lesions leave dogs able to avoid obstacles while failing to recognize familiar objects. | `neuropsychology` |
| 340 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1881 | Max Friedrich: First Doctoral Dissertation in Wundts Lab | Max Friedrich & Wilhelm Wundt | Completes first PhD thesis on the duration of apperception using hipp chronoscopes in Leipzig. | `experimental_psych` |
| 341 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1881 | Theodule Ribot: Diseases of Memory and Ribots Law | Theodule Ribot, Paris | Law of retrograde amnesia: brain lesions dissolve recent memories first, preserving oldest habits longest. | `neuropsychology` |
| 342 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1882 | C.S. Peirce & Joseph Jastrow: Minimum Perceptible Difference | Charles Sanders Peirce & Joseph Jastrow, Hopkins | Introduces randomized blinded psychophysical trials and proves subliminal weights bias forced choices. | `experimental_psych` |
| 343 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1882 | Charcot: Grande Hysterie and Three Stages of Hypnosis | Jean-Martin Charcot, Paris | Classifies hypnotic states into lethargy, catalepsy, and somnambulism, claiming hypnosis is morbid hysteria. | `clinical_psych` |
| 344 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1882 | Society for Psychical Research Founded in London | Henry Sidgwick & Frederic Myers | Investigates telepathy, mesmerism, and apparitions, pioneering early scientific scrutiny of unconscious minds. | `philosophy_of_mind` |
| 345 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1882 | Wilhelm Preyer: The Mind of the Child Published | Wilhelm Preyer, Jena Germany | Pioneers systematic developmental observational methodology, recording daily motor and language milestones. | `developmental_psych` |
| 346 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1883 | Angelo Mosso: The Human Circulation Balance Machine | Angelo Mosso, Turin | Balances human on tipping table, showing blood rushes to head when subject engages in complex mental arithmetic. | `neuropsychology` |
| 347 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1883 | Emil Kraepelin: Compendium of Psychiatry Diagnostic System | Emil Kraepelin, Heidelberg | Establishes medical nosology classifying mental disorders by clinical course: dementia praecox vs mood swings. | `clinical_psych` |
| 348 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1883 | Francis Galton: Inquiries into Human Faculty and Eugenics | Francis Galton, London | Coins the term eugenics, pioneering mental imagery questionnaires, composite photography, and anthropometry. | `experimental_psych` |
| 349 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1883 | G. Stanley Hall: First Formal Research Lab at Johns Hopkins | G. Stanley Hall, Baltimore MD | Founds first dedicated psychology research laboratory in the United States modeled on Leipzig. | `experimental_psych` |
| 350 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1884 | Galtons Anthropometric Laboratory at London Health | Francis Galton, South Kensington | Over 9,000 visitors pay to measure visual acuity, reaction time, hearing pitch, and physical strength. | `experimental_psych` |
| 351 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1884 | Krafft-Ebing: Legal Responsibility in Mental Alienation | Richard von Krafft-Ebing, Graz | Pioneers clinical assessment of diminished capacity and irresistible impulse in criminal madness trials. | `clinical_psych` |
| 352 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1884 | Magnus Blix & Alfred Goldscheider: Cutaneous Skin Spots | Magnus Blix (Uppsala) & Goldscheider (Berlin) | Independently map distinct punctuate skin receptors dedicated to warmth, cold, pressure, and pain. | `experimental_psych` |
| 353 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1884 | William James: What is an Emotion? Published | William James, Mind Journal | Formulates James-Lange theory: bodily visceral changes precede emotion (we feel sorry because we cry). | `experimental_psych` |
| 354 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1885 | Carl Lange: Vasomotor Theory of Emotional States | Carl Lange, Copenhagen | Independently publishes physiological theory attributing emotional feelings to vascular blood flow shifts. | `experimental_psych` |
| 355 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1885 | Ebbinghaus: Overlearning and Long-Term Memory Retention | Hermann Ebbinghaus, Berlin | Demonstrates additional learning repetitions beyond mastery yield disproportionately durable memory traces. | `cognitive_psych` |
| 356 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1885 | Georges Gilles de la Tourette: Motor and Vocal Tic Study | Georges Gilles de la Tourette, Salpêtrière | Describes nine patients with childhood-onset motor tics, involuntary vocalizations, and echolalia. | `neuropsychology` |
| 357 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1885 | Hermann Ebbinghaus: Memory and the Forgetting Curve | Hermann Ebbinghaus, Berlin | Pioneers quantitative memory research on himself: nonsense syllables (CVCs), savings score, and spacing effect. | `cognitive_psych` |
| 358 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1885 | Jean-Martin Charcot: Hysteria Demonstrations at Salpetriere | Jean-Martin Charcot, Paris | Tuesday lessons present grand hysteria patients, linking hysterical anesthesia to hypnotic susceptibility. | `clinical_psych` |
| 359 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1886 | Ernst Mach: The Analysis of Sensations and Mach Bands | Ernst Mach, Prague | Analyzes retinal lateral inhibition creating optical gradient borders, founding radical empiricist physics. | `experimental_psych` |
| 360 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1886 | Hippolyte Bernheim: Suggestive Therapeutics (Nancy School) | Hippolyte Bernheim, Nancy France | Rejects Charcots neurological hysteria model, proving hypnotic phenomena stem entirely from normal suggestion. | `clinical_psych` |
| 361 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1886 | James McKeen Cattell: Word Reading vs Color Naming Speed | James McKeen Cattell, Leipzig | Proves reading words aloud is faster than naming color patches, foreshadowing Stroop automaticity. | `experimental_psych` |
| 362 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1886 | Victor Horsley: Cortical Resection for Focal Epilepsy | Victor Horsley, Queen Square London | First successful surgical resection of motor cortex scar tissue guided by intraoperative electrical stimulation. | `neuropsychology` |
| 363 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1887 | Cattell: Letter Legibility and Tachistoscopic Reading | James McKeen Cattell, Leipzig & UPenn | Measures word recognition latency, showing readers grasp whole familiar words faster than isolated letters. | `cognitive_psych` |
| 364 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1887 | G. Stanley Hall: Founds The American Journal of Psychology | G. Stanley Hall, Baltimore | First English-language scientific periodical devoted exclusively to publishing laboratory psychological research. | `experimental_psych` |
| 365 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1887 | Richard von Krafft-Ebing: Psychopathia Sexualis | Richard von Krafft-Ebing, Vienna | Pioneering forensic psychopathology textbook classifying paraphilias, sadomasochism, and sexual deviance. | `clinical_psych` |
| 366 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1887 | Sergei Korsakoff: Amnestic Confabulation Syndrome | Sergei Korsakoff, Moscow Imperial Univ | Describes dense anterograde amnesia and vivid confabulation in chronic alcohol-induced brain damage. | `neuropsychology` |
| 367 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1888 | Féré & Tarchanoff: Electrodermal Skin Response (GSR) | Charles Féré (Paris) & Ivan Tarchanoff | Discover emotional arousal and sensory stimuli alter electrical skin conductance via sweat glands. | `experimental_psych` |
| 368 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1888 | James McKeen Cattell: Professor of Psychology at UPenn | James McKeen Cattell, Philadelphia | First university chair in the world designated explicitly as Professor of Psychology. | `experimental_psych` |
| 369 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1888 | Santiago Ramón y Cajal: The Neuron Doctrine Established | Santiago Ramón y Cajal, Barcelona | Proves the nervous system consists of discrete contiguous cells rather than a continuous syncytial net. | `neuropsychology` |
| 370 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1888 | Yujiro Motora: First Psychology Laboratory in Japan | Yujiro Motora, Tokyo Imperial Univ | Student of Hall and Wundt establishes first Japanese academic experimental psychology laboratory. | `cultural_psych` |
| 371 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1889 | First International Congress of Physiological Psychology | Charcot, Ribot & James, Paris | Convenes over 200 international scientists to formalize psychology as an autonomous global scientific field. | `experimental_psych` |
| 372 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1889 | Franz Carl Müller-Lyer: The Finned Line Length Illusion | Franz Carl Müller-Lyer, Munich | Introduces the famous arrowhead optical illusion where inward vs outward fins distort perceived length. | `experimental_psych` |
| 373 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1889 | Henri Beaunis: First French Laboratory at Sorbonne | Henri Beaunis & Alfred Binet, Paris | Establishes laboratory of physiological psychology to investigate hypnosis, memory, and cognitive reaction times. | `experimental_psych` |
| 374 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1889 | Pierre Janet: LAutomatisme Psychologique and Dissociation | Pierre Janet, Paris | Discovers psychological trauma causes subconscious dissociation and splitting of conscious personality. | `clinical_psych` |
| 375 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1889 | Vladimir Bekhterev: Kazan Psychophysiological Laboratory | Vladimir Bekhterev, Kazan Russia | Establishes Russia's first experimental psychology and neuroanatomy lab, founding Reflexology. | `experimental_psych` |
| 376 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1890 | Christian von Ehrenfels: On Gestalt Qualities | Christian von Ehrenfels, Prague | Discovers melody retains perceptual identity when transposed to different key; whole exceeds sum of parts. | `cognitive_psych` |
| 377 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1890 | Gabriel Tarde: The Laws of Imitation in Society | Gabriel Tarde, Paris | Argues social behavior, crime, and cultural innovations spread through interpersonal imitation waves. | `social_psych` |
| 378 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1890 | Hugo Munsterberg: Voluntary Action and Muscle Feedback | Hugo Munsterberg, Freiburg | Argues conscious feeling of will is sensory awareness of preliminary bodily tensions and motor movements. | `experimental_psych` |
| 379 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1890 | James McKeen Cattell: Mental Tests and Measurements | James McKeen Cattell, Mind Journal | Coins the term mental tests, measuring sensorimotor capacities to assess undergraduate intellectual aptitude. | `experimental_psych` |
| 380 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1890 | William James: The Principles of Psychology Published | William James, Harvard University | Monumental masterwork defining stream of consciousness, habit, attention, emotion, and the multi-layered self. | `philosophy_of_mind` |
| 381 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1891 | Edward Scripture: Psychological Laboratory at Yale | Edward Wheeler Scripture, Yale | Pioneers brass instrument precision measurements of speech phonetics, reaction time, and athletic skill. | `experimental_psych` |
| 382 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1891 | Heinrich Wilhelm Waldeyer: Coining the Term Neuron | Heinrich Wilhelm Waldeyer, Univ of Berlin | Synthesizes Cajal's histological evidence to coin the word Neuron for the basic unit of the brain. | `neuropsychology` |
| 383 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1891 | Mary Whiton Calkins: Psychology Lab at Wellesley College | Mary Whiton Calkins, Wellesley MA | Founds first womens college psychological laboratory, studying association, memory, and dreams. | `experimental_psych` |
| 384 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1891 | Sigmund Freud: On Aphasia and Coining Agnosia | Sigmund Freud, Vienna | Neurological monograph critiquing strict cortical localization and introducing the term Agnosia. | `neuropsychology` |
| 385 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1892 | American Psychological Association (APA) Founded at Clark | G. Stanley Hall & 31 Founders | Establishes professional organization to advance psychology as a recognized scientific discipline in USA. | `experimental_psych` |
| 386 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1892 | Christine Ladd-Franklin: Evolutionary Theory of Color Vision | Christine Ladd-Franklin, Baltimore | Proposes color vision evolved in stages from black-white to blue-yellow and finally red-green sensitivity. | `evolutionary_psych` |
| 387 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1892 | Edward B. Titchener Arrives at Cornell: Structuralism | Edward Bradford Titchener, Ithaca NY | Brings Wundtian introspectionism to America, dissecting conscious experience into raw sensory atoms. | `experimental_psych` |
| 388 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1892 | Joseph Jastrow: The Bistable Duck-Rabbit Illusion | Joseph Jastrow, Univ of Wisconsin | Uses the ambiguous duck-rabbit figure to prove visual perception is active mental construction. | `cognitive_psych` |
| 389 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1893 | Columbian Exposition Psychology Exhibit in Chicago | Joseph Jastrow & Hugo Munsterberg | Over two million visitors view working laboratory apparatus demonstrating reaction times and illusions. | `experimental_psych` |
| 390 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1893 | Harry Kirke Wolfe & E.W. Scripture: Size-Weight Illusion | Augustin Charpentier & E.W. Scripture | Demonstrates a smaller object feels significantly heavier than a larger object of identical physical mass. | `experimental_psych` |
| 391 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1893 | Oswald Kulpe: Outlines of Psychology Published | Oswald Kulpe, Leipzig | Synthesizes experimental psychophysics and reaction time studies, laying groundwork for Wurzburg school. | `experimental_psych` |
| 392 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1894 | Alfred Binet: Cognitive Study of Blindfold Chess Masters | Alfred Binet, Sorbonne Paris | Proves chess grandmasters rely on abstract semantic spatial schemas rather than photographic memory. | `cognitive_psych` |
| 393 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1894 | C. Lloyd Morgan: Morgans Canon in Animal Psychology | C. Lloyd Morgan, Bristol | Parsimony rule: animal behavior must not be interpreted as higher mental faculty if lower process suffices. | `evolutionary_psych` |
| 394 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1894 | Georg Elias Müller: Memory Drum and Retroactive Interference | G.E. Müller & F. Schumann, Gottingen | Invents the mechanized memory drum and discovers new learning interferes with consolidating old lists. | `cognitive_psych` |
| 395 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1894 | John Dewey: The Ego as Cause and Evolutionary Psychology | John Dewey, Univ of Michigan | Functionalist critique arguing mind evolved to resolve real environmental obstacles and social challenges. | `philosophy_of_mind` |
| 396 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1894 | Margaret Floy Washburn: First Woman Awarded Psychology PhD | Margaret Floy Washburn, Cornell | Graduates under Titchener, later pioneering comparative animal cognition in The Animal Mind. | `experimental_psych` |
| 397 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1894 | William James: The Physical Basis of Emotion Defended | William James, Psychological Review | Answers critics of James-Lange emotion model, detailing autonomic visceral pathways and motor feedback. | `experimental_psych` |
| 398 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1895 | Gustave Le Bon: The Crowd: A Study of the Popular Mind | Gustave Le Bon, Paris | Influential social treatise asserting crowds dissolve individual rationality into suggestible collective mania. | `social_psych` |
| 399 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1895 | Mary Whiton Calkins: Inventing Paired-Associate Learning | Mary Whiton Calkins, Harvard | Invents paired-associate memory paradigm to prove frequency of exposure dominates cognitive recall. | `cognitive_psych` |
| 400 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1895 | Titchener: The Postulates of a Structural Psychology | Edward B. Titchener, Cornell | Contrasts structural psychology (mental anatomy) with functional psychology (mental physiology). | `experimental_psych` |
| 401 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1896 | Alfred Binet & Victor Henri: Individual Psychology Paper | Alfred Binet & Victor Henri, Paris | Shifts differential psychology from simple sensory discrimination to higher memory and imagination. | `cognitive_psych` |
| 402 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1896 | G. Stanley Hall: The Child Study Movement Sweeps America | G. Stanley Hall, Clark Univ | Mass distribution of developmental questionnaires tracking childrens fears, play, and moral ideas. | `developmental_psych` |
| 403 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1896 | John Dewey: The Reflex Arc Concept in Psychology | John Dewey, Univ of Chicago | Manifesto of functionalism: reflex is not a segmented stimulus-response chain but an integrated functional circuit. | `philosophy_of_mind` |
| 404 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1896 | Lightner Witmer: World First Psychological Clinic at UPenn | Lightner Witmer, Philadelphia | Coins the term clinical psychology, treating children with learning disabilities and speech impediments. | `clinical_psych` |
| 405 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1896 | Max von Frey: Esthesiometer Hairs and Pain Receptors | Max von Frey, Leipzig | Calibrates horsehair esthesiometers to measure exact cutaneous touch thresholds and nociceptor spots. | `experimental_psych` |
| 406 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1897 | Charles Sherrington: Coining the Term Synapse | Sir Charles Scott Sherrington, Liverpool | Coins the word Synapse in Foster's Textbook of Physiology for the functional junction between neurons. | `neuropsychology` |
| 407 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1897 | Emile Durkheim: Suicide and Social Integration | Emile Durkheim, Bordeaux France | Groundbreaking sociological psychology linking suicide rates to social anomie, isolation, and integration. | `social_psych` |
| 408 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1897 | George Malcolm Stratton: Inverted Vision Prism Goggles | George Malcolm Stratton, UC Berkeley | Wears upside-down retinal inversion lenses for eight days, proving cortical visual-motor adaptation. | `experimental_psych` |
| 409 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1897 | Norman Triplett: First Social Psychology Experiment | Norman Triplett, Indiana University | Proves cyclists pedal faster in competition: discovers social facilitation phenomenon in human performance. | `social_psych` |
| 410 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1897 | Wundt: Outlines of Psychology and Tridimensional Emotion | Wilhelm Wundt, Leipzig | Maps feelings along three continuous axes: pleasure-displeasure, strain-relaxation, and excitement-calm. | `experimental_psych` |
| 411 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1898 | Edward Thorndike: Animal Intelligence and Puzzle Boxes | Edward Thorndike, Columbia Univ | Places cats in wooden puzzle boxes, formulating the Law of Effect: behaviors yielding satisfaction are repeated. | `behaviorism` |
| 412 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1898 | Hermann Ebbinghaus: Sentence Completion Intelligence Test | Hermann Ebbinghaus, Breslau | Develops sentence completion task to measure intellectual fatigue in Breslau school children. | `cognitive_psych` |
| 413 | 4. Birth of Scientific Psychology | 1898 | Thorndike: Law of Exercise and Stamping Out Behaviors | Edward Thorndike, New York | Proves connections are strengthened by repeated practice and extinguished when unrewarded in animal trials. | `behaviorism` |
